\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}).\nIn contrast, canary$\\to$diversity pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24).\nWithout the differencing diagnostic, an analyst would conclude that canaries predict perplexity, a conclusion entirely driven by co-trending rather than temporal precedence.\nThe danger lies in the selective nature of this confound: only by applying the diagnostic can one determine which channels are genuine and which are artifacts of shared non-stationarity.\n\n\\paragraph{Pitfall 3: Regime dependence.}\nEven after fixing the statistical method, the contamination level~$\\alpha$ determines whether a lead-lag relationship is detectable.\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced, BH FDR-corrected at $q{=}0.05$) find 6/8 pairs FDR-significant at both $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (2~fp32 seeds, consistent) and $\\alpha{=}1.0$, but 0/8 through $\\alpha{=}0.45$ (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}).\nThe transition zone at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.45$--$0.50$ is where method divergence is most dangerous: correlation-based methods find significance across all levels while causal methods remain null through $\\alpha{=}0.45$ (a faint single-pair signal appears at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, FDR $p{=}0.053$, but does not reach aggregate detection), creating the maximum risk of spurious conclusions when only a single (liberal) method is used.\nThis regime dependence is consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short ecological time series \\cite{boettiger2012limits}, and suggests that detectability boundaries should be explicitly mapped rather than assumed.\n\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Method Consensus Framework}\n\\label{sec:consensus}\n\nThe pitfalls above motivate a multi-method consensus approach inspired by specification curve analysis \\cite{simonsohn2020specification} and multi-method validation in early-warning signal detection \\cite{scheffer2009early}: rather than relying on any single method, we apply multiple independent methods to quantify how analytic choices determine conclusions, using agreement thresholds as diagnostic benchmarks.\n\n\\paragraph{Formal definition.}\nGiven $M$ lead-lag detection methods $\\{m_1, \\ldots, m_M\\}$ and $N$ independent random seeds, a canary$\\to$downstream pair $(X, Y)$ is classified as \\textsc{robust} if and only if:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item At least $K$ of $M$ methods judge the pair FDR-significant, where each method independently applies Benjamini--Hochberg correction at $q{=}0.05$; and\n    \\item At least $S$ of $N$ seeds replicate this conclusion.\n\\end{enumerate}\nThe thresholds $(K, S)$ control the trade-off between false positive and false negative rates.\nMethods with different inductive biases (correlation-based methods that detect any temporal association versus Granger-type methods that test directional predictive improvement \\cite{granger1969investigating}) provide complementary evidence.\nTheir convergence on a given pair constitutes stronger evidence than agreement among methods sharing the same statistical assumptions.\nWe note that Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not true causality \\cite{shojaie2022granger}; our framework inherits this limitation.\n\n\\paragraph{Method tiering.}\nThe five methods are organized into two tiers based on empirical reliability.\nThree \\emph{primary} methods---naive cross-correlation, differenced cross-correlation, and permutation Granger causality---produce consistent results across independent analysis pipelines and exhibit adequate statistical power at $T{=}11$ generations.\nTwo methods are classified as \\emph{supplementary}: threshold onset detection due to power limitations at $T{=}11$ under corrected $3\\sigma$ calibration (\\S\\ref{sec:threshold_limitation}), and Toda--Yamamoto Granger causality, which achieves moderate detection (3--4/8 at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$) but with reduced power relative to the primary methods (\\S\\ref{sec:ty_limitation}).\nThe consensus panel thus comprises four contributing methods (three primary, one moderate supplementary), with threshold onset near-inoperative at $T{=}11$.\nThe consensus threshold $K{\\geq}3$ out of 5 methods requires agreement among at least the three primary methods or two primary methods plus TY, making $K{\\geq}3$ a \\emph{stronger} standard than a simple majority vote would suggest.\n\n\\paragraph{Sequential filtering pipeline.}\nIn practice, the $K$-of-$M$ voting operates over all five methods applied independently to each pair; the sequential pipeline below describes the order in which results are examined, enabling early termination and interpretable diagnostics at each stage (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}):\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Naive correlation scan.} Cross-correlation at maximum lag~5 with BH correction serves as a permissive baseline; all pairs with any temporal association pass.\n    \\item \\textbf{First-differencing diagnostic.} Cross-correlation is recomputed on first-differenced series, testing whether temporal associations survive removal of shared linear trends. Pairs that lose significance are flagged as potential spurious regressions.\n    \\item \\textbf{Permutation Granger causality.} Permutation-based Granger causality tests \\cite{shojaie2022granger} on first-differenced series (5{,}000 permutations, BH correction at $q{=}0.05$) assess directional predictive improvement: whether past values of $X$ improve prediction of $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history.\n    \\item \\textbf{Multi-seed replication.} The conclusion must hold across $S$ of $N$ independent training seeds, filtering seed-specific artifacts.\n\\end{enumerate}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, this pipeline progressively filters: 16 pairs pass Stage~1, 14 survive Stage~2 (differencing removes 2 marginal canary$\\to$perplexity pairs), 6 survive Stage~3 (Granger testing eliminates remaining canary$\\to$perplexity pairs and failed canary candidates), and all~6 survive Stage~4 (multi-seed replication confirms all Granger-significant pairs).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/consensus_workflow.pdf}\n\\caption{Sequential filtering pipeline of the multi-method consensus framework. Starting from all candidate canary$\\to$downstream pairs, the pipeline applies four stages: (1)~naive cross-correlation scan (permissive baseline), (2)~first-differencing diagnostic (removes shared-trend artifacts), (3)~permutation Granger causality (tests directional predictive improvement), and (4)~multi-seed replication (filters seed-specific artifacts). At $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M), the pipeline reduces 16 candidate pairs to 6 robust pairs: 16$\\to$14$\\to$6$\\to$6. The $K$-of-$M$ consensus vote operates in parallel across all five methods; the sequential ordering provides interpretable diagnostics at each stage.}\n\\label{fig:consensus_workflow}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Practical guidance.}\nThe consensus thresholds $(K, S)$ should be chosen to match the analyst's tolerance for false positives versus false negatives.\nTable~\\ref{tab:practical_guidance} provides four recommended tiers, calibrated against our experimental controls (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\nTier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$) achieves the best trade-off under the full 5-method panel: zero false positives while recovering all six reference pairs; however, threshold onset's near-zero power at $3\\sigma$ (\\S\\ref{sec:threshold_limitation}) makes it near-inoperative, and Toda--Yamamoto provides moderate supplementary signal (3--4/8 at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$; \\S\\ref{sec:ty_limitation}), making $K{\\geq}3$ the operationally relevant consensus threshold.\nWith four contributing methods (three primary plus TY), $K{\\geq}3$ draws on a broader evidence base than the three primary methods alone; TY's moderate detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$ strengthens consensus conclusions where it contributes a positive vote.\nCross-architecture validation confirms $K{\\geq}3$ reaches ${\\geq}6/8$ in 10 of 14 architecture-seed combinations (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nMethod subset ablation confirms that this threshold is uniquely optimal: at $K{=}3/5$, the null-control FPR is 37.5\\% (unacceptable); at $K{=}4/5$, FPR drops to 0\\% while retaining 6/6 reference-pair sensitivity; at $K{=}5/5$, FPR remains 0\\% but sensitivity falls to 3/6.\nShapley value analysis (exact enumeration of all 32 coalitions; Figure~\\ref{fig:shapley}) identifies naive cross-correlation as the sole positive contributor to FPR (Shapley(FPR)${=}{+}0.348$) and permutation Granger as the strongest FPR suppressor (Shapley(FPR)${=}{-}0.194$), the only method combining 0\\% null-control FPR with high sensitivity.\nToda--Yamamoto's Shapley contribution (FNR${=}{-}0.158$) reflects moderate supplementary value: it reduces consensus FNR without introducing false positives (\\S\\ref{sec:ty_limitation}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/shapley_method_contribution.pdf}\n\\caption{Shapley value decomposition of method contributions to consensus FPR and FNR (exact enumeration of all $2^5{=}32$ coalitions, $K{=}\\max(1,|S|{-}1)$). FPR is computed at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (3~seeds $\\times$ 8~pairs${=}$24 observations); FNR at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4~seeds, 6 reference pairs). Naive cross-correlation is the only positive FPR contributor (${+}0.348$); permutation Granger is the strongest FPR suppressor (${-}0.194$). Toda--Yamamoto contributes moderate FNR suppression (Shapley FNR${=}{-}0.158$) as a supplementary method (\\S\\ref{sec:ty_limitation}).}\n\\label{fig:shapley}\n\\end{figure}\nTier~4 requires unanimity across methods and seeds, which incurs elevated false negative rates; TY's moderate detection (3--4/8) means that requiring $5/5$ unanimity is substantially more restrictive than $4/5$, particularly at intermediate contamination levels where TY may not contribute a positive vote.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Practical guidance tiers for lead-lag consensus analysis. Tier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$, $S{\\geq}3/4$) is uniquely optimal under the full 5-method panel; with threshold onset power-limited (\\S\\ref{sec:threshold_limitation}) and Toda--Yamamoto providing moderate supplementary signal (\\S\\ref{sec:ty_limitation}), $K{\\geq}3$ draws on four contributing methods. FPR is calibrated at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (null control, $M{=}5$ methods, $N{=}3$ seeds, Pythia-410M). FNR is calibrated at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ against 6 effect-size reference pairs using a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation); $N{=}4$ seeds (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).}\n\\label{tab:practical_guidance}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nTier & Use Case & $K/M$ & $S/N$ & FPR & FNR \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & Exploratory     & ${\\geq}1/5$ & ${\\geq}1/N$ & 100\\%  & 0\\%    \\\\\n2 & Screening       & ${\\geq}3/5$ & ${\\geq}2/N$ & 37.5\\% & 0\\%    \\\\\n3 & Confirmatory    & ${\\geq}4/5$ & ${\\geq}3/N$ & 0\\%    & 0\\%    \\\\\n4 & Conservative    & $5/5$       & $N/N$        & 0\\%    & 50\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Empirical Calibration}\n\\label{sec:calibration}\n\nWe calibrate the framework using two built-in experimental controls.\nFull numerical results are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}.\n\n\\paragraph{False positive rate.}\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.0$ condition serves as a null control: no synthetic data is injected, so any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nAt $K{=}1$ (any single method suffices), FPR${=}$75\\%: naive cross-correlation flags 6/8 forward-direction pairs.\nAt $K{=}2$, FPR drops to ${\\leq}$37.5\\%; at $K{=}3$, to ${\\leq}$12.5\\%; and at $K{\\geq}4$, FPR${=}$0\\%.\nThe consensus framework at Tier~3 matches the false positive performance of the best individual method (permutation Granger, 0\\%) while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method.\n\n\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$ with 4~seeds, we define six \\emph{effect-size reference pairs} using criteria independent of the consensus methods:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item[(a)] The canary$\\to$downstream drift ratio at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ must exceed $3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline drift (canary ratios: entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$, both far above threshold); and\n    \\item[(b)] Canary onset must strictly precede downstream onset by at least one generation (canary onset: generation~1; diversity onset: generations~3--9).\n\\end{enumerate}\nThese criteria yield token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1, distinct-2, and distinct-3~\\cite{li2016diversity}.\nThe perplexity channel is excluded because its onset (generation~1) coincides with canary onset, consistent with, but not derived from, the differencing diagnostic finding that canary$\\to$perplexity associations are spurious (\\S\\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}).\nThe effect-size threshold is robust: at $2\\times$, $3\\times$, and $5\\times$ the baseline drift, the reference set remains identical (6~pairs), because canary ratios ($11.93\\times$ and $73.12\\times$) far exceed all thresholds.\n\nFNR is calibrated using a 4-method panel excluding differenced cross-correlation, which shares statistical properties with detrending-based criteria and could introduce circularity.\nAt $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$, all six reference pairs are recovered (FNR${=}$0\\%).\nAt $K{=}4/4$, $S{=}4/4$, FNR rises to 50\\%, reflecting TY's moderate detection power (4/8 at $\\alpha{=}1.0$; \\S\\ref{sec:ty_limitation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Symmetric calibration.}\nBoth FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\\times}$seed dimension: FPR across 3~seeds at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (0/24 pairs at $K{\\geq}3$, $3\\sigma$) and FNR across 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (6/6 reference pairs recovered at $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$).\nCross-seed FPR variance is negligible: all three null-control seeds yield 0/8 robust pairs at $K{\\geq}3$ ($3\\sigma$), confirming seed-independent performance.\n\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold onset detection: power limitations at $3\\sigma$.}\n\\label{sec:threshold_limitation}\nPost-hoc validation of the threshold onset implementation (M028) revealed that the published $2\\sigma$ calibration was mislabeled as $3\\sigma$.\nUnder corrected $3\\sigma$ calibration, threshold onset yields 0/8 significant pairs in 8 of 12 cross-architecture conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}) and at most 2/8 in the remainder, reducing it to a near-zero-power method at $T{=}11$ generations; any threshold onset detections should therefore be interpreted as lower-bound estimates.\nThis low power is expected: with only 10 generation-over-generation differences, the null variance estimate has few degrees of freedom, and a $3\\sigma$ exceedance threshold becomes overly conservative.\nWe retain threshold onset as a supplementary method for completeness and report its results transparently, but adopt permutation Granger forward significance as the primary detection indicator in cross-architecture analyses.\n\n\\paragraph{Toda--Yamamoto Granger: moderate supplementary signal.}\n\\label{sec:ty_limitation}\nPost-hoc analysis identified a statsmodels API incompatibility that initially produced artificially low Toda--Yamamoto detection rates.\nAfter correcting the implementation, TY achieves moderate detection: 3/8 forward-direction pairs at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (seed~42), 3/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, and 4/8 at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}).\nThese rates are lower than permutation Granger (6--8/8) but confirm that TY captures genuine signal at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$, consistent with the method's theoretical motivation~\\cite{toda1995statistical}.\nTY is classified as a moderate supplementary method: it contributes to the consensus panel but does not serve as a primary detection indicator due to reduced statistical power at $T{=}11$ (the augmentation order $d_{\\max}$ consumes additional degrees of freedom) and sensitivity to the $d_{\\max}$ hyperparameter (Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}b).\nWith TY providing moderate signal, the consensus panel comprises four contributing methods (three primary plus TY as supplementary), strengthening the $K{\\geq}3$ threshold: at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (seed~42), $K{\\geq}3$ rises from 5/8 to 6/8 with TY's corrected contribution.\nAt $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$, $K{\\geq}4$ consensus (4 of 5 methods agree) emerges in 3/8 pairs, demonstrating that multi-method agreement extends beyond the primary trio.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Method Consensus Test (MMCT)}\n\\label{sec:mmct}\n\nThe uniform threshold ($K{\\geq}4/5$) achieves FPR${=}$0\\% and FNR${=}$0\\% on reference extremes (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}), but this ad-hoc threshold lacks formal statistical grounding: null-calibrated permutation testing assigns $p{=}0.16$ to $K{\\geq}4/5$, failing to reach significance at conventional levels.\nThe threshold also treats all methods equally, despite the heterogeneous error profiles documented in \\S\\ref{sec:pitfalls}: naive cross-correlation has 75\\% forward-direction null-control FPR while permutation Granger has 0\\%.\nWe formalize the \\emph{Multi-Method Consensus Test} (MMCT), a weighted consensus framework that assigns each method influence proportional to its empirical specificity, following the specification curve tradition~\\cite{simonsohn2020specification} in which analytical choices are systematically varied and aggregated rather than arbitrarily fixed~\\cite{bell2022modeling}.\nMMCT serves as an evaluation framework rather than an end in itself: its primary function is to enable systematic comparison of detection methods under controlled conditions, identifying which individual methods are reliable and why others fail.\nWe do not claim a novel statistical method; MMCT is a consensus testing protocol adapted to the specific constraints of iterative training analysis: short series ($T{=}5$--$20$), non-stationarity by design, and heterogeneous method sensitivities.\n\n\\paragraph{FPR-inverse weighting.}\nLet $\\hat{\\text{FPR}}_k$ denote the empirical false positive rate of method $m_k$, estimated from $N_0$ null-control observations at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ across $S$ seeds.\nEach method receives weight\n\\begin{equation}\n    w_k = \\frac{1}{\\max(\\hat{\\text{FPR}}_k,\\; \\epsilon)},\n    \\label{eq:fpr_weight}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\epsilon{=}0.01$ prevents division by zero.\nMethods with low FPR (e.g., permutation Granger at 0\\%, receiving $w_k{=}100$) dominate the consensus; methods with high FPR (e.g., naive cross-correlation at 75\\%, receiving $w_k{\\approx}1.3$) are effectively downweighted.\n\n\\paragraph{Consensus score.}\nGiven $M$ methods that return binary decisions $d_k \\in \\{0, 1\\}$ (FDR-significant or not) for a candidate pair $(X, Y)$, the weighted consensus score is:\n\\begin{equation}\n    W(X \\to Y) = \\frac{\\sum_{k=1}^{M} w_k \\cdot d_k}{\\sum_{k=1}^{M} w_k}.\n    \\label{eq:consensus_score}\n\\end{equation}\nWhen all weights equal~1 (uniform mode), $W$ reduces to $K/M$, and thresholding at $W \\geq 4/5$ recovers the uniform $K{\\geq}4$ rule exactly.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical testing.}\nThe null distribution of $W$ is estimated from the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ control condition, where no synthetic contamination is present and any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nThe $N_0$ null-control pairs yield a reference distribution $\\{W_0^{(j)}\\}_{j=1}^{N_0}$, and the MMCT $p$-value is:\n\\begin{equation}\n    p_{\\text{MMCT}} = \\frac{|\\{j : W_0^{(j)} \\geq W_{\\text{obs}}\\}|}{N_0}.\n    \\label{eq:pvalue}\n\\end{equation}\nWe reject the null of no lead-lag relationship at significance level $\\alpha_{\\text{test}}$.\n\n\\paragraph{Bidirectional filtering.}\nMMCT tests both directions independently: $W(X \\to Y)$ and $W(Y \\to X)$.\nA stricter bidirectional mode requires forward significance with reverse non-significance.\nEmpirically, bidirectional and forward-only detection rates are identical across all contamination levels tested, indicating that pairs reaching weighted consensus in the forward direction consistently fail to reach consensus in reverse.\nWe therefore adopt forward-only (weighted pair forward) as the default MMCT configuration.\nAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:mmct} summarizes the procedure.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Multi-Method Consensus Test (MMCT)}\n\\label{alg:mmct}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\Require Methods $\\{m_1, \\ldots, m_M\\}$; pair $(X, Y)$; null pairs $\\{(X_0^{(j)}, Y_0^{(j)})\\}_{j=1}^{N_0}$; floor $\\epsilon$; level $\\alpha_{\\text{test}}$\n\\Ensure Decision $d$, score $W$, $p$-value\n\\Statex \\textbf{Phase 1: Estimate weights from null control}\n\\For{$k = 1, \\ldots, M$}\n    \\State $\\hat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k \\gets$ null-control rejection rate of $m_k$\n    \\State $w_k \\gets 1 / \\max(\\hat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k, \\epsilon)$\n\\EndFor\n\\Statex \\textbf{Phase 2: Compute weighted consensus}\n\\For{$k = 1, \\ldots, M$}\n    \\State $d_k \\gets m_k(X, Y)$ \\Comment{1 if FDR-significant}\n\\EndFor\n\\State $W \\gets \\sum_k w_k d_k \\;/\\; \\sum_k w_k$\n\\Statex \\textbf{Phase 3: Null-distribution $p$-value}\n\\For{$j = 1, \\ldots, N_0$}\n    \\State $W_0^{(j)} \\gets$ consensus score of $(X_0^{(j)}, Y_0^{(j)})$\n\\EndFor\n\\State $p \\gets |\\{j : W_0^{(j)} \\geq W\\}| / N_0$\n\\State \\Return $\\mathbb{1}[p < \\alpha_{\\text{test}}]$, $W$, $p$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n\\paragraph{Empirical comparison.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:mmct_ablation} compares per-method detection rates across contamination levels, revealing the systematic comparison that motivates our method recommendation.\nThe five methods exhibit sharply divergent dose-response profiles: naive cross-correlation maintains elevated FPR at the null (75\\%) but rises to 87.5\\% at $\\alpha{=}0.75$; differenced cross-correlation achieves zero null-control FPR but detects in both forward and reverse directions, failing to provide directional separation; threshold onset detects only at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (50\\%) and returns to zero at $\\alpha{=}0.75$ under $3\\sigma$ calibration; Toda--Yamamoto achieves moderate detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$ (37.5\\%) with zero null-control FPR but lower sensitivity than permutation Granger.\nPermutation Granger alone provides the cleanest dose-response profile, with a sharp step function from 0/8 (blind spot, $\\alpha{\\leq}0.45$) to 6/8 (onset, $\\alpha{=}0.50$, dual-seed confirmed) and reliable detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$ (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}).\n\nPermutation Granger produces binary per-pair verdicts that yield a sharp phase transition in detection: a faint signal at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (best pair FDR $p{=}0.053$) that does not reach aggregate detection through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then an abrupt jump to 6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (fp32), with a non-monotonic dip at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ (s42: 3/8, s43: 0/8; dual-seed confirmed synchronous collapse zone; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}).\nThis step-function profile, combined with zero null-control FPR and Shapley FPR suppression of $-0.194$, identifies PermG as the recommended individual detection method.\nCross-architecture validation confirms PermG fwd ${\\geq}6/8$ in 12 of 14 conditions, with multi-method consensus ($K{\\geq}3$) reaching ${\\geq}6/8$ in 10 of 14 (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nMMCT weighted consensus further captures graded detection strength even where individual methods remain below threshold, providing formal $p$-values that the uniform $K$-of-$M$ threshold lacks; however, the systematic comparison reveals that PermG alone provides comparable or superior detection to the full consensus framework at all contamination levels tested.\nWith TY contributing as a fourth method, full 5/5 consensus becomes achievable: at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (seed~42), the ECE$\\to$distinct-3 pair reaches significance across all five methods, demonstrating that the consensus panel can converge unanimously even near the onset boundary when the canary--downstream coupling is strong.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Per-method forward-direction detection rates across contamination levels (Pythia-410M, seed~42, fp32). Each cell reports the fraction of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs reaching BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$). Per-method dose-response reveals the systematic comparison that identifies PermG as the recommended method (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}). Cross-condition results at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:mmct_cross}. $^\\dagger$Moderate supplementary method (\\S\\ref{sec:ty_limitation}); FPR$_\\text{null}$ pending (null condition not yet run through corrected pipeline).}\n\\label{tab:mmct_ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & FPR$_\\text{null}$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr              & 75   & 12.5 & 37.5 & 87.5 \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr             & 0    & 37.5 & 87.5 & 75   \\\\\nThreshold ($3\\sigma$)    & ---  & 0    & 50   & 0    \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger           & 0    & 0    & 75   & 75   \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto$^\\dagger$ & ---  & 0    & 37.5 & 37.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Backward compatibility.}\nSetting all weights $w_k{=}1$ reduces MMCT to the uniform $K$-of-$M$ threshold, making the existing consensus framework (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus}) a special case.\nDescriptively, $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves FPR${=}$0\\% and FNR${=}$0\\% on reference extremes (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}); however, null-calibrated testing assigns $p{=}0.16$ to this threshold, indicating that it does not reach conventional significance levels.\nMMCT weighted consensus resolves this gap by providing formal $p$-values (Eq.~\\ref{eq:pvalue}) grounded in the empirical null distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-condition stability.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:mmct_cross} reports MMCT weighted consensus across all seven experimental conditions at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nDetection ranges from 4/8 to 8/8 while FPR remains 0 at the null control across all conditions, confirming that FPR-inverse weighting generalizes beyond the calibration setup.\nGPT-2-medium achieves the highest detection rate (8/8), consistent with its stronger per-method signal, demonstrating that MMCT's FPR-inverse weighting extracts graded detection strength that binary per-method verdicts miss.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{MMCT weighted forward consensus across experimental conditions ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, FPR-inverse weighting). Each cell reports significant canary$\\to$downstream pairs out of 8 (2~canaries $\\times$ 4~downstream metrics). All conditions maintain FPR${=}$0 at $\\alpha{=}0.0$. Values may be updated pending parameter consistency verification.}\n\\label{tab:mmct_cross}\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Sig / 8 \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline (Pythia-410M / Wiki)     & 5/8 \\\\\nArchitecture: GPT-2-medium / Wiki & \\textbf{8/8} \\\\\nArchitecture: OPT-1.3B / Wiki     & 5/8 \\\\\nScale: Pythia-1.4B / Wiki (s42)   & 4/8 \\\\\nScale: Pythia-1.4B / Wiki (s43)   & 4/8 \\\\\nDomain: Pythia-1B / C4 (s42)      & 5/8 \\\\\nDomain: Pythia-1B / C4 (s43)      & 4/8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Theoretical Motivation: Why Entropy Leads Diversity}\n\\label{sec:theory}\n\nThe empirical lead-lag relationship between token entropy and lexical diversity (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}) admits a simple theoretical motivation grounded in the distinct mathematical properties of continuous and discrete summary statistics.\nWe present three arguments that, taken together, predict the observed temporal ordering and its dependence on contamination strength.\nThese arguments provide intuition rather than formal proof; the dose-response experiments in \\S\\ref{sec:regime} test the resulting predictions empirically.\n\n\\paragraph{Continuous versus discrete response.}\nToken entropy $H(p_t^{(g)}) = -\\sum_v p_v \\log p_v$ is a continuous functional of the token distribution $p_t^{(g)}$ at generation $g$: any infinitesimal shift in $p_t^{(g)}$ produces a proportional change in $H$.\nDistinct-$n$ diversity $D_k^{(g)}$, by contrast, is a counting statistic.\nAn $n$-gram contributes to $D_k^{(g)}$ only when its probability exceeds an effective sampling threshold $\\tau_n \\approx 1/N$, where $N$ is the number of generated tokens.\nBelow $\\tau_n$, the $n$-gram is absent from finite samples regardless of its true probability.\n$D_k^{(g)}$ therefore responds to distributional change in discrete steps: it remains constant while probabilities drift within the interval above $\\tau_n$, then drops abruptly when a probability crosses the threshold.\nBecause $H$ tracks every distributional perturbation while $D_k^{(g)}$ remains silent until a threshold crossing occurs, entropy is sensitive to distributional change strictly before diversity registers it.\n\n\\paragraph{Four predicted detectability intervals.}\nThe continuous-versus-discrete asymmetry predicts four intervals of the contamination parameter $\\alpha$, ordered by increasing signal strength:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Blind spot} ($\\alpha$ small).\nThe distributional shift is too weak for $H$ to exceed noise-level variation, and no $n$-gram probabilities cross $\\tau_n$.\nBoth metrics show null response.\n\\item \\textbf{Canary-only window} ($\\alpha$ moderate-low).\n$H$ registers a statistically significant shift, but the probability mass redistribution has not yet pushed enough $n$-grams below $\\tau_n$ to alter $D_k^{(g)}$.\nEntropy signals collapse; diversity does not.\n\\item \\textbf{Lead-lag regime} ($\\alpha$ moderate-high).\n$H$ continues to lead, and $D_k^{(g)}$ begins to respond with a measurable delay as $n$-gram probabilities progressively cross $\\tau_n$.\nThe temporal gap between the two is detectable by Granger-type tests.\n\\item \\textbf{Synchronous collapse} ($\\alpha$ high).\nThe distributional shift is so large that $H$ and $D_k^{(g)}$ change within the same generation, collapsing the lead to zero and rendering temporal precedence undetectable.\n\\end{enumerate}\nThese four intervals align with the empirical dose-response profile: a blind spot at $\\alpha \\leq 0.45$, onset of detection at $\\alpha = 0.50$, reliable lead-lag at $\\alpha \\geq 0.75$, and a synchronous dip at $\\alpha = 0.60$ where entropy and diversity shift near-simultaneously (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}).\nWe emphasize that this mapping is a qualitative prediction, not a quantitative derivation; the interval boundaries depend on model capacity, dataset properties, and sample size $N$.\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to effective rank.}\nThe link between entropy and diversity can be made more concrete through the notion of effective rank~\\texttt{[CITE]}.\nFor a probability distribution $P$, the effective rank $\\mathrm{erank}(P) = \\exp(H(P / \\|P\\|_1))$ measures the number of dimensions that carry substantial probability mass.\nA decline in $H$ corresponds to a contraction of $\\mathrm{erank}(P)$: the distribution concentrates on fewer tokens.\nAs concentration increases, tokens in the tail of $P$ fall below the sampling threshold $\\tau_n$, and the $n$-grams they participate in vanish from generated text.\nThis yields a causal chain: $H$ decreases $\\to$ $\\mathrm{erank}$ contracts $\\to$ tail probabilities cross $\\tau_n$ $\\to$ $D_k^{(g)}$ drops.\nEntropy sits at the upstream end of this chain, which is why it responds first.\nThe chain also clarifies why the lead vanishes at extreme $\\alpha$: when collapse is sudden enough, all three stages occur within a single generation, eliminating the temporal separation that makes lead-lag detection possible.",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the complete dose-response for PermG forward significance:

| α | PermG fwd | K≥3 | Notes |
|---|-----------|------|-------|
| 0.00 | 0/8 | 0/8 | null control (from D124) |
| 0.10 | 0/8 | ? | |
| 0.25 | 0/8 | ? | |
| 0.45 | 0/8 | ? | |
| 0.50 | 6/8 | 6/8 | onset ✓ |


| 0.60 s43 | 0/8 | 0/8 | sync dip ✓ |
| 0.75 | 6/8 | 6/8 | recovery ✓ |
| 1.0 | 6/8 | 6/8 | reliable ✓ |

The pattern is unmistakably non-monotonic—at α=0.60 there's a sharp dip back to 0/8 before climbing back to 6/8 at higher values. This is the critical finding.

Now I'm seeing a fundamental mismatch between the theory and what's actually happening. The theory predicts four regimes that should increase monotonically with α, culminating in synchronous collapse at the highest values. But the data shows synchronous collapse happening at intermediate α (around 0.60), then recovery at higher α values (0.75 and beyond). The theory's explanation for why collapse occurs at high α doesn't match where it's actually appearing in the empirical results.

What's particularly problematic is that the theory explicitly states collapse happens when α is high and all three stages compress into a single generation—yet at extreme α values like 1.0, the performance actually recovers to 6/8 PermG. The roadmap seems to acknowledge this non-monotonic pattern correctly, but the theory subsection is written as if the progression should be strictly monotonic, which creates a logical contradiction that any reviewer would immediately flag. incorrectly by assuming monotonic ordering.

Let me also check: does line 314 actually claim a monotonic ordering? Line 314: "These four intervals align with the empirical dose-response profile: a blind spot at α ≤ 0.45, onset of detection at α = 0.50, reliable lead-lag at α ≥ 0.75, and a synchronous dip at α = 0.60 where entropy and diversity shift near-simultaneously."

The listing order is: blind → onset → reliable → sync. But α values are 0.45 → 0.50 → 0.75 → 0.60. The text deliberately lists sync last and reliable before it, perhaps to downplay the non-monotonicity. But this is misleading — a reader would expect the listing to follow α ordering.

More importantly, the theory itself (lines 299-313) clearly lists them in monotonic α order: "Four predicted detectability intervals...ordered by increasing signal strength: (1) Blind spot (α small), (2) Canary-only window (α moderate-low), (3) Lead-lag regime (α moderate-high), (4) Synchronous collapse (α high)."

But here's the critical problem: the theory predicts synchronous collapse at high α, yet the data shows it peaks at α=0.60 with recovery at α=1.0. This is a fundamental mismatch that a reviewer would flag immediately — the theory doesn't account for why the collapse reverses at higher signal strengths. Now I need to check whether the canary-only window prediction is even empirically supported. is too weak to detect with the Granger test, even though the underlying effect might exist. This is more of an empirical detection limitation than a theoretical problem, but the paper should flag this nuance. Let me verify the effective rank citation — Roy & Vetterli, 2007 is indeed the standard reference — and then I'll compile my full review.
```

[severity: critical] **理论预测的单调排序与实验数据的非单调 dose-response 矛盾**。\n\n   理论（lines 299-313）明确预测 4 个区间按 α 单调递增排列：Blind(α small) → Canary-only(moderate-low) → Lead-lag(moderate-high) → **Synchronous collapse(α high)**。第 4 个区间被描述为\"α high...The distributional shift is so large that H and D_k change within the same generation\"。\n\n   但服务器验证的实际 dose-response 是非单调的：\n   - α=0.50: PermG=6/8（onset）\n   - α=0.60 s43: **PermG=0/8**（sync dip — 在中间 α，不是最高）\n   - α=0.75: PermG=6/8（恢复）\n   - α=1.0: PermG=6/8（可靠检测）\n\n   **矛盾核心**：理论说 sync collapse 发生在\"α high\"（最强信号），但实验显示它在 α=0.60（中间值），α=1.0 反而有 6/8 的可靠检测。理论的单调框架无法解释 PermG 在 α=0.60 骤降后 α=0.75 恢复这一非单调现象。\n\n   line 324 的表述更加误导：\"The chain also clarifies why the lead vanishes at extreme α\"——但 extreme α (1.0) 恰恰是 lead 最可靠被检测的区间（PermG 6/8），lead 消失的是在 intermediate α (0.60)。\n\n   审稿人攻击面：**\"Your theory predicts synchronous collapse at extreme contamination, yet your data shows it at intermediate α with recovery at higher α. How do you reconcile this non-monotonicity?\"** 这是一个论文级别的逻辑漏洞。\n\n2. [severity: warning] **line 314 通过非 α 排序暗淡化非单调性**。文本列举顺序为\"blind→onset→reliable→sync dip\"，但 α 值为 0.45→0.50→0.75→0.60——故意将 sync dip 放在最后而非其自然的 α 顺序位置。这不是欺骗但会被仔细阅读的审稿人发现叙述回避了核心矛盾。\n\n3. [severity: warning] **\"Canary-only window\"（lines 305-307）缺少直接实验验证**。理论预测某 α 区间内 entropy 有信号但 diversity 无响应。但：\n   - α=0.25 的 PermG=0/8（entropy→diversity Granger 无信号）\n   - effective_rank 在 α=0.25 有 FDR 显著信号（p_FDR=0.029），但那是 effective_rank→diversity，不是 token_entropy→diversity\n   - 如果 canary-only window 存在于 α∈[0.10, 0.45]，论文需要用除 PermG 之外的证据展示 entropy 在此范围确实有可检测的异常\n\n4. [severity: warning] **`\\texttt{[CITE]}` 占位符未解决（line 318）**。Roy & Vetterli 2007 的 effective rank 引用缺失。references.bib 需补充。提交前必须修复。\n\n5. CCS / Multispatial CCM 可用实现\n\n### CCS (Convergent Cross Sorting, Breston 2021)\n\n| 项目 | 详情 |\n|------|------|\n| **官方实现** | [lbreston/CCS](https://github.com/lbreston/CCS) — **MATLAB only**，5个文件（`CCS.m`, `convergence.m`, `fnnTDsweep.m`），8 stars |\n| **Python 实现** | **不存在。** PyPI 无 CCS 包，GitHub 无 Python port |\n| **最短测试长度** | 论文测试到 L=50，未测 L<50。T=11 未经验证 |\n| **论文** | [Scientific Reports 2021](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-021-98864-2)；[限制性分析 (2023)](https://royalsocietypublishing.org/doi/10.1098/rsos.221590) 指出 CCS 在极短序列上的限制 |\n\n### Multispatial CCM (Clark et al. 2015)\n\n| 项目 | 详情 |\n|------|------|\n| **R 实现** | [multispatialCCM](https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/multispatialCCM/) — R 包，CRAN 上可用，[GitHub](https://github.com/adamtclark/multispatialCCM) |\n| **Python 实现** | **不存在。** 无 Python port |\n| **核心思路** | 将多个短序列（spatial replicates）拼接为一个长向量（NA 分隔），对拼接后序列做 CCM + 自举检验 |\n| **最短长度** | 论文验证 T≥5 可行（需要多个 spatial replicates） |\n| **适用性** | **高度适配我们场景**：我们有 N 个 seed/layer 的短序列（T=11 或 T=20），可视为 spatial replicates |\n| **论文** | [Clark et al. 2015, Ecology](https://esajournals.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1890/14-1479.1) |\n\n### 其他 Python CCM 包（均不支持 CCS/Multispatial）\n\n| 包 | 状态 | 备注 |\n|----|------|------|\n| [skccm](https://pypi.org/project/skCCM/) | vanilla CCM only | scikit-learn 风格 API，不支持 multispatial |\n| [causal_ccm](https://github.com/PrinceJavier/causal_ccm) | vanilla CCM + PAI | 不支持 CCS/multispatial |\n| [pyCCM](https://github.com/C-Naoki/pyCCM) | vanilla CCM | 同上 |\n| [ccmMul](https://github.com/SharathSPhD/ccmMul) | multivariate CCM（多变量，非 multispatial） | 不同概念 |\n| pyccm (PyPI) | vanilla CCM, Python 2.7 | 过时 |\n\n---\n\n## 2. 自行实现的可行性评估\n\n### CCS Python 实现（~2-3天工作量）\n\n从 MATLAB 源码提取的核心算法：\n\n```\nCCS(x, y, lag, tau, dim, thresh):\n  1. 延迟嵌入重构: 构建 Le×dim 矩阵 X, Y\n     Le = L - 1 - (dim-1)*tau,  行 i: [x[i+T], x[i+T-tau], ..., x[i]]\n  2. 距离矩阵: dX = pdist2(X,X), dY = pdist2(Y,Y)  (Le×Le)\n  3. 去对角线: 用 std*thresh 阈值掩码移除临近点\n  4. 展平+排序: xflat, yflat → 独立排序获取 rank (归一化到 [0,1])\n  5. 交叉排序: r_xcy = X的rank按Y的排序重排, r_ycx 反之\n  6. NERR²: se = (r_xcy - rank)², null = rank² - rank + 1/3\n     RVP = cumsum(-(se - null) / null) / count\n  7. 指数拟合: f(x) = a + b*exp(c*x), 加权最小二乘\n  8. 耦合分数: C = f(0)，即 y 截距，裁剪到 [-1, 1]\n```\n\n**依赖**: numpy, scipy（`cdist`, `curve_fit`）\n\n**风险**: T=11 时 Le = 11 - 1 - (E-1)*tau。若 E=2, tau=1 → Le=9，距离矩阵仅 9×9=81 个元素（去对角线后更少），排序统计量极不稳定。**CCS 在 T=11 上大概率不可靠。**\n\n### Multispatial CCM Python 实现（~1-2天，推荐）\n\n核心思路更简单：\n1. 将 K 个短序列（每个 T=11）拼接，NA 分隔 → 有效长度 ~K*11\n2. 对拼接序列做标准 CCM（跳过 NA 邻居）\n3. 自举显著性检验\n\n**可直接基于 skccm 修改**，只需加 NA-aware 的距离计算和拼接逻辑。我们有 ~40+ seeds × multiple layers，spatial replicates 充足。\n\n**推荐方案**: 实现 Multispatial CCM wrapper 包裹 skccm，工作量最小且理论上对 T=11 场景最适配。\n\n---\n\n## 3. 三篇论文关键内容\n\n### 3.1 Gu et al. — 未找到精确匹配\n\n搜索 \"Gu 2025 ICLR spotlight cross-lingual alignment emergence\" 未找到完全匹配的论文。最接近的候选：\n\n- **Jiawei Gu et al. \"CMR Scaling Law\"** (EMNLP 2024)：发现 loss、mixture ratio、training tokens 之间的 power-law 关系，定义了 Critical Mixture Ratio (CMR)。但这是关于 continual pre-training 而非 cross-lingual alignment emergence。[arXiv:2407.17467](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.17467)\n- **\"Data Mixing Can Induce Phase Transitions in Knowledge Acquisition\"** (2025)：critical mixing ratio 遵循与 model size 的 power law。[arXiv:2505.18091](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2505.18091)\n- **Wang et al. \"Probing the Emergence of Cross-lingual Alignment during LLM Training\"** (ACL Findings 2024)：观察到 cross-lingual neuron overlap 与下游性能高度相关。[arXiv:2406.13229](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.13229)\n\n**需要确认**: Director 引用的论文标题/作者可能有偏差，请核实。\n\n### 3.2 Truong & Truong (2025) — Entropy Collapse\n\n**论文**: [Entropy Collapse: A Universal Failure Mode of Intelligent Systems](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.12381)\n**作者**: Truong Xuan Khanh & Truong Quynh Hoa\n\n**关键公式**:\n\n| 概念 | 公式 |\n|------|------|\n| 临界阈值 | α_c(β) = 1/(1-β)，α=反馈放大，β=有界新颖性再生 |\n| 序参量 | m = 1 - H_ss/H_max |\n| Class 判定 | κ = f″(1/N)，κ>0 → Class 1，κ=0 → Class 2 |\n\n**两个 Universality Classes**:\n\n| | Class 1 (κ>0, convex) | Class 2 (κ=0, linear) |\n|---|---|---|\n| 相变类型 | 一阶不连续 | 二阶连续 |\n| 熵跳变 | Δm₀ = 0.698 | 0 |\n| 迟滞 | ΔH_hyst ≈ 2.73 nats | 无 |\n| 弛豫指数 | ν = 1 | ν = 1/2 |\n| 可逆性 | **不可逆** | **可逆** |\n| 例子 | 所有 power-law 反馈、multiplicative weights | 线性反馈 |\n\n**与我们场景的对应**:\n- 我们的 entropy collapse（训练中 token distribution diversity 下降）属于 **Class 1**（softmax 是 convex feedback）\n- 一阶相变意味着 **collapse 无渐进预警**（CSD indicators 可能失效），与我们 CSD 检验结果一致\n- 不可逆性解释了为什么 collapse 后无法恢复\n\n### 3.3 Dakos et al. (2012) — CSD 方法论综述\n\n**论文**: [Methods for Detecting Early Warnings of Critical Transitions](https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0041010), PLoS ONE 7(7): e41010\n\n**核心方法清单**:\n\n**Metric-based（滑动窗口内计算）**:\n| 指标 | 检测什么 | 公式/方法 |\n|------|----------|-----------|\n| AR(1) | 自相关增强（critical slowing down 核心信号）| lag-1 自相关系数 |\n| Variance / SD | 方差增大 | 滚动窗口内二阶矩 |\n| Skewness | 分布不对称 | 三阶标准化矩 |\n| Kurtosis | 尾部变厚 | 四阶标准化矩 |\n| Spectral reddening | 低频功率增加 | 功率谱分析 |\n| DFA | 长程相关 | 去趋势波动分析（需 >100 数据点） |\n\n**趋势检验**: Kendall τ 秩相关，接近 1 = 强增趋势\n\n**显著性检验**: \n1. 对去趋势残差拟合 ARMA(p,q)\n2. 生成 1000 个 surrogate 序列\n3. 比较原始 Kendall τ 与 surrogate 分布\n4. 跨所有参数组合（窗口大小、带宽）做敏感性分析\n\n**与我们场景的对应**:\n- 我们 T=11 远低于 DFA 要求的 >100 → DFA 不可用\n- 滑动窗口法需要足够长的序列 → 窗口=half length=5 点，统计量不可靠\n- 这正是为什么需要 Multispatial CCM（利用多 seed/layer replicates 弥补单序列太短的问题）\n- Truong 的 Class 1 结论（一阶相变无渐进预警）从理论上解释了 CSD 在我们场景失效的根因\n\n---\n\n## 总结建议\n\n**因果检验推荐路径**: 实现 Multispatial CCM Python wrapper（基于 skccm + NA-aware 拼接），利用我们丰富的 seed/layer replicates。CCS 对 T=11 风险太高（距离矩阵太小）。\n\n**Gu 论文**: 未找到精确匹配，需 Director 确认具体标题或 arXiv ID。"

```text
Now I'm evaluating whether the connection between Class 1 first-order transitions and the absence of gradual early warning actually holds up scientifically. The step function behavior we see at α=0.50 and the non-monotonic dip at α=0.60 could both be explained by first-order transition dynamics—the sudden onset matches discontinuous behavior, and the hysteresis pattern fits with irreversibility. But I need to verify that Truong & Truong's framework actually applies to our entropy collapse system, and I'm also concerned about a missing citation to the Gu paper that I should track down. systems" entropy collapse — is it directly applicable to our iterative training scenario?
- The mapping α → their α_c(β) parameter needs to be validated
- "softmax is convex feedback → Class 1" is a claim that needs verification

For my review, I should confirm the worker's scientific assessments are sound, flag the missing Gu paper citation, and note the potential connection between Truong's Class 1 and the theory subsection's issues where the non-monotonic dip could relate to hysteresis in first-order transitions. Model Scale: 无单调关系**\n- Qwen-0.8B (7.0) ≈ Pythia-6.9B (7.0) > Pythia-410M (6.0) > 1.4B (4.0) > SmolLM3-3B (3.0)\n- 弱化根因是 entropy trajectory shape（U 型/饱和），不是 model size\n- ECE canary 在所有模型上比 entropy 更鲁棒\n\n**4. vs Gu et al.**: α_c≈0.48 是 detection threshold 不是 collapse threshold；iterative compounding 下 effective contamination 在 α=0.45 和 0.50 几乎相同（99.88% vs 99.95%），但 PermG 从 0 跳到 6。两者互补但不可直接比较。\n\n**5. 缺口**: α=0.60 无 canonical 数据，等 M046 canonical sweep。\n\n图表位于 `westd-17855:/root/autodl-tmp/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts_phase_transition/`。"
```

```text
This is D127 Track D — Phase Transition analysis. Key findings:

1. **Sigmoid fit α_c=0.476, steepness k≥200, transition width Δα≈0.02**: This is a strong finding. The PermG onset is near-discontinuous.
2. **Sigmoid >> Power Law (ΔAIC=18.2)**: Strong model selection result.
3. **Model scale has no monotonic relationship with detection strength**: Interesting - Qwen-0.8B ≈ Pythia-6.9B > Pythia-410M > 1.4B > SmolLM3-3B. This weakens any claim about scale dependence.


4. **α=0.60 canonical data gap**: Worker correctly identifies this gap — v4 is still running. The "sharp onset of causal detectability" framing is reasonable and avoids overclaiming a first-order phase transition without sufficient evidence.

Now I'm checking whether the sigmoid parameters align with the observed data — the jump from 0/8 detections at α≤0.45 to 6/8 at α=0.50 does place the midpoint around 0.476, and the steepness parameter indicates an extremely sharp transition.

I need to verify the 3-seed consistency at α=0.50 and examine the sigmoid fit more carefully: how many α values were used, and whether alternative models like a step function might actually fit better than the sigmoid despite the ΔAIC=18.2 favoring it over power law.

The model scale finding is interesting—it suggests trajectory shape matters more than size itself, which would actually weaken claims about larger models being inherently more vulnerable. I should also wait for the v4 run on cuda:5 to complete so I can test whether that non-monotonic dip at α=0.60 holds up with the canonical data.

The connection to Truong & Truong's work is compelling: the steep crossover with k≥200 and Δα≈0.02 does align with their Class 1 prediction, and the Worker's shift toward "sharp onset of causal detectability" rather than "phase transition" is the right call scientifically—true phase transitions need infinite-size limits. Let me pull the artifacts from the server to validate the sigmoid fit directly.
```

_source line 6569 · 2026-05-20T09:22:23.953Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I see a critical issue. The script reads `5method_leadlag.json` files — NOT the D120 canonical files (`d105_d111_joint_3sigma.json` or the M046-updated `5method_leadlag.json`). Line 27: `for jf in sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(base, 'alpha_sweep/*/analysis/5method_leadlag.json'))):`.

This is the SAME non-canonical file problem I've flagged multiple times. The `5method_leadlag.json` files in the alpha_sweep directory may or may not have been updated by M046. On 17855, M046 showed "无变化" for the core alpha sweep experiments, so the existing files should be fine. But I should verify.
 
The real question is whether the alpha_sweep directory files were already processed through the D120 unified analysis pipeline by M046, or if they're still using the older format. M046 specifically targeted experiments that hadn't been run through D120 yet, so the alpha_sweep files might still need verification.

Looking at the script's logic, it's pulling `5method_leadlag.json` files from the alpha_sweep directory via glob, but that pattern won't capture the α=1.0 data stored separately in the generalize directory. The script manually adds that α=1.0 data as a workaround, but I need to verify whether s42 at α=1.0 is being included correctly or if there's a gap in the coverage. So the fit is using `permg_mean` which averages across seeds, meaning at α=1.0 only s43's value of 6 gets included since s42 isn't captured by the glob pattern. This reduces the sample size but doesn't affect the mean since both seeds show 6/8 anyway. It's a minor issue that probably doesn't impact the fit result, though I should keep it in mind. Let me trace through the actual data points the script is using.
```

</details>
